\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;,

(142)

we see that the cohomology of
B_{1}
corresponds to field configurations that satisfy the linear field equations modulo linearized gauge transformations, which correspond to the theory’s physical or propagating degrees of freedom. Consequently, to understand the relation between the physics of the full theory and that of the subspaces, we need to understand the relation between the cohomologies of the cochain complexes
(B_{1},\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}})
and
(\bar{B}_{1},\bar{\mathcal{X}})
with
\bar{\mathcal{X}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}}
, where
\bar{B}_{1}
is the differential that describes the theory in the desired regime, i.e, at the desired power of the radial coordinate
r
. In the subsequent general discussion, we follow
[
70
]
.

The first step in establishing the relation between the physics of the full theory and that of the subspaces is to introduce a projection map
\pi:\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}}\to\bar{\mathcal{X}}
which acts diagrammatically as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \bar{X}_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{1}\;.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}

(143)

If the projection map
\pi
obeys the following relation:

\pi\circ B_{1}=\bar{B}_{1}\circ\pi\;,

(144)

then we say that the
\pi
is a
cochain map
. Diagrammatically, we say that
\pi
is a cochain map if the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{i+1}\;,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}

(145)

for all
i
. In words, the diagram commutes if, starting from the space
X_{i}
, we can follow either of the two possible routes to
\bar{X}_{i+1}
and obtain the same result. Naturally, cochain maps induce maps on the cohomologies
H^{i}
and
\bar{H}^{i}
of the differentials
B_{1}
and
\bar{B}_{1}
, respectively, as we shall prove in the following. Consider an element
x\in H^{i}
, that is,
x\sim x+B_{1}(y)
, where
\sim
indicates that they are equivalent in
H^{i}
. We denote the whole equivalence class as
[x]
. The projection map acts on an arbitrary representative of
H^{i}

[x+B_{1}(y)]
as

\begin{split}\bar{H}^{i}\ni[\pi(x+B_{1}(y))]&=[\pi(x)+\pi(B_{1}(y))]\\
&=[\pi(x)+\bar{B}_{1}(\pi(y))]\\
&=[\pi(x)]\;,\end{split}

(146)

where in the second line we used the cochain map condition (
144
) and to get to the last line we used the fact that, in the cohomology
\bar{H}^{i}
,
\pi(x)\sim\pi(x)+\bar{B}_{1}(\pi(y))
. The above computation proves that, indeed,
\pi
induces a well-defined, representative independent map on cohomologies, which, in general, is non-invertible. Physically,
\pi
takes an arbitrary element of the full theory in
\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}}
and projects it to the regime we wish to consider. As we shall define in detail in the following sections, for our purposes,
\pi
truncates powers of the radial coordinate
r
to obtain expansions in this coordinate of the form (
14
).

In this paper, we also consider a map
\iota:\bar{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}}
called the
inclusion map
that acts diagrammatically as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \bar{X}_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{1}\;,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(147)

which tells us what the elements of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
look like in the original, bigger space
\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}}
. To induce a map in cohomologies, analogously to
\pi
,
\iota
shall obey the cochain map condition

B_{1}\circ\iota=\iota\circ\bar{B}_{1}\;.

(148)

Thus, the inclusion map
\iota
induces a map inverse to that induced by the projection map
\pi
in cohomologies, i.e.,

\begin{split}H^{i}\ni[\iota(\bar{x}+\bar{B}_{1}(\bar{y}))]&=[\iota(\bar{x})+\iota(\bar{B}_{1}(\bar{y}))]\\
&=[\iota(\bar{x})+B_{1}(\iota(\bar{y}))]\\
&=[\iota(\bar{x})]\;,\end{split}

(149)

with
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}
. In other words, there exists an isomorphism on cohomologies and we say that there exists a
quasi-isomorphism
between the two chain complexes
(B_{1},\mathcal{X}^{\rm{SDYM}})
and
(\bar{B}_{1},\bar{\mathcal{X}})
. Physically, this indicates that both chain complexes contain the same physical information.

7.2
Cochain map conditions

The goal is to construct a new
L_{\infty}
-algebra
(\bar{\mathcal{X}},\bar{B}_{1},\bar{B}_{2})
that encodes the physics of the initial theory near
\mathcal{I}
. To this end, we shall define two maps
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}
and
\iota:\bar{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{B}_{1}}

(150)

commutes. In other words, we require
\pi
and
\iota
to be cochain maps. In general, the first two brackets on
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
are defined in terms of the brackets on
\mathcal{X}
via the cochain maps as

\bar{B}_{i}:=\pi B_{i}\iota,\quad i=1,2\;.

(151)

We observe that

\mathrm{\eqref{homotopy transfer diagram}\ commutes}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\bar{B}_{1}\pi=\pi B_{1}\\
B_{1}\iota=\iota\bar{B}_{1}\end{cases}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\mathrm{Im}(1_{\mathcal{X}}-\iota\pi)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\pi B_{1})\\
\mathrm{Im}(B_{1}\iota)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{X}}-\iota\pi)\end{cases}\;,

(152)

where in the second equivalence we used the definition of
\bar{B}_{1}
. We want
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
to satisfy an equation similar to (
92
), namely

\bar{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{n\leq 0}\subset\mathcal{X}\;.

(153)

In particular, we require
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
to be a subspace of
\mathcal{X}
. Hence, the maps
\pi,\iota
shall be, respectively, the projection to
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
and the canonical inclusion:

\begin{aligned} \pi\colon\mathcal{X}&\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{X}}\\
\psi&\mapsto\pi(\psi)\;,\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \iota\colon\bar{\mathcal{X}}&\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}\\
\bar{\psi}&\mapsto\bar{\psi}\;.\end{aligned}

(154)

Notice that for an arbitrary
\psi\in\mathcal{X}
, we can write
\iota\pi(\psi)=\pi(\psi)
, where both sides of this equality are viewed as elements of
\mathcal{X}
due to (
153
). In particular,

1_{\mathcal{X}}-\iota\pi=1_{\mathcal{X}}-\pi=:\pi^{\complement}\;,

(155)

where
\pi^{\complement}
is the projection to the subspace complementary to
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
(see discussion in
section

5.1
). Moreover,
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Im}\pi\oplus\mathrm{Im}\pi^{\complement}
, where
\mathrm{Im}\pi=\bar{\mathcal{X}}
. Of course,
\mathrm{Im}\pi^{\complement}=\mathrm{Ker}\pi
(and this identity is symmetric under
\pi\leftrightarrow\pi^{\complement}
). This implies that (
150
) commutes iff

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\pi^{\complement}\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\pi B_{1})

(156a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(B_{1}\iota)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}\pi^{\complement}=\mathrm{Im}\pi=\bar{\mathcal{X}}\;.

(156b)

In particular, the second inclusion above implies that the subspace
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
is closed under the action of the differential
B_{1}
. Thus,
\bar{B}_{1}=B_{1}|_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}}
.

In
section

5
we showed that (
153
) is solved by

\bar{\mathcal{X}}:=\{\psi\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\ |\ \mathcal{N}\ \mathrm{satisfies\ \eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N}}\}\;,

(157)

where
\mathcal{N}
is the collection of power selection sets introduced in (
109
) and
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is the morphism defined in (
111
). In other words,
\pi=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
. In what follows, we will prove that there is only one choice of sets in
\mathcal{N}
such that (
156b
) with
\pi=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is satisfied. (For this reason,
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
in (
157
) does not carry the label
\mathcal{N}
.)

7.3
Fall-off from cochain map conditions

Result 7.1
.

Let
D
be the diagram in (
150
) with
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
as in (
157
), projection
\pi=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
and canonical inclusion
\iota
. Then
D
commutes iff the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}
are

\displaystyle N_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=N_{A_{\alpha}}=N_{E_{uz}}=\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}

(158)

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle=N_{E_{ru}}=N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}=\mathbb{Z}^{<0}\setminus\{-1\}.

Proof.

From equation (
156b
) it follows that

D\ \mathrm{commutes}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1})\\
\mathrm{Im}(B_{1}\iota)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}\end{cases}\Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi)\coloneqq\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}(\psi)=0&\forall\psi\in\mathcal{X}\\
\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{\psi})\coloneqq\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}B_{1}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{\psi})=0&\forall\bar{\psi}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}\;,\end{cases}

(159)

where
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}\coloneqq 1_{\mathcal{X}}-\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is the projection to the complement of
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
and we used
\iota(\bar{\psi})=\bar{\psi}=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{\psi})
. We want to find the conditions that the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}
must obey so that the second system above holds. For the first equation of this system, the answer is provided by lemma
A.2
of
appendix

A
. There, we prove that
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi)
vanishes for all
\psi\in\mathcal{X}
iff the sets in
\mathcal{N}
satisfy the inclusions listed in (
172
). To solve the second equation in (
159
), we observe that
\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}
can be obtained from
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}
by switching
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\leftrightarrow\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}
. Moreover, from (
111
) and (
174
) it follows that
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}=\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{\complement}}
, where
\mathcal{N}^{\complement}
is the collection of sets containing the complements in
\mathbb{Z}
of the sets in
\mathcal{N}
. Therefore, we deduce that
\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\Psi_{\mathcal{N}^{\complement}}
. So, the system in (
159
) is solved by a projector
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with
\mathcal{N}
such that both
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\complement}
satisfy the inclusions in (
172
). Such a collection
\mathcal{N}
consists of sets satisfying the system:

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}=\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus\{-1\}

(160a)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\alpha}}=N_{\Lambda}

(160b)

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}=N_{A_{r}}=\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}=\mathcal{T}_{-2}N_{A_{z}}=\mathcal{T}_{-2}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

(160c)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}=N_{A_{r}}

(160d)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}=\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}

(160e)

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}=N_{A_{u}}=N_{A_{z}}\;,

(160f)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the translation defined in (
115
). To find the above equations, we used the following fact: given
M,N,L\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, one can prove that

i
)

if
M\setminus\{m\}\subseteq N
and
M^{\complement}\setminus\{m\}\subseteq N^{\complement}
, then
M\setminus\{m\}=N\setminus\{m\}
;

ii
)

if
L\subseteq M\cap N
and
L^{\complement}\subseteq M^{\complement}\cap N^{\complement}
, then
L=M=N
.

At this point, it is easy to see that (
158
) is the only possible solution for both (
114c
) (which is our hypothesis) and the system (
160f
).
∎

Equation (
158
) provides a proof of the fall-offs given in (
14
); thus, we are effectively proving the same result already established in
section

5
, but in a more concise and elegant way. Moreover, by comparing the power selection sets found solving the master system in
section

5
and the ones found here, we observe that

\bar{\mathcal{X}}=\lim_{k\rightarrow-\infty}\mathcal{X}^{(k)}\;,

(161)

cf. equations (
158
) and (
117
).

7.4
(\bar{\mathcal{X}},\bar{B}_{1},\bar{B}_{2})
is an
L_{\infty}
sub-algebra

Lemma 7.2
.

\bar{B}_{2}=B_{2}|_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}}

Proof.

From the definition of
\bar{B}_{2}
, see (
151
), it follows that the result we want to prove is equivalent to the statement that
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
is closed under the action of
B_{2}
27
27

27

We observe that this is equivalent to saying that
B_{2}
commutes with the inclusion
\iota
, since
\mathrm{Im}[B_{2}(\iota\otimes\iota)]\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}=\mathrm{Ker}(1_{\mathcal{X}}-\iota\pi_{\mathcal{N}})
iff
B_{2}(\iota\otimes\iota)=\iota\bar{B}_{2}
.
, i.e.

\mathrm{Im}[B_{2}(\iota\otimes\iota)]\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{X}}=\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathrm{Ker}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}\;.

(162)

In other words, it suffices to show that
\Xi\coloneqq\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}B_{2}(\iota\otimes\iota)=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}B_{2}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{N}})
vanishes on
\bar{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{X}}
. This is always true, as one can show using the explicit form of
B_{2}
and the properties of the projectors
28
28

28

In particular, use (
166g
).
.
For instance, for arbitrary
\bar{\Lambda}_{1},\bar{\Lambda}_{2}\in\bar{X}_{-1}
,

\Xi(\bar{\Lambda}_{1},\bar{\Lambda}_{2})=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{>0}}[\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\bar{\Lambda}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\bar{\Lambda}_{2}]=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{>0}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}[\bar{\Lambda}_{1},\bar{\Lambda}_{2}]=0\;.

(163)

Analogously, one can prove that
\Xi(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})
vanishes for all other choices of
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}
.
∎

This implies that
(\bar{\mathcal{X}},\bar{B}_{1},\bar{B}_{2})
is an
L_{\infty}
sub-algebra of
(\mathcal{X},B_{1},B_{2})
. In appendix
C
, we provide a proof of this result based on the concept of
homotopy transfer
which consists of the transfer of algebraic information between different chain complexes. Moreover, we show how the kinematic algebra for these choices of fall-offs can be constructed.

8
Conclusions

In this article, we have employed the power of homotopy algebras to construct robust truncations of self-dual Yang-Mills in a
\frac{1}{r}
expansion near null infinity. We have found that the physical fall-off of gauge fields can be
derived
from the requirement that slices preserve the
L_{\infty}
relations of the original theory. We then proceeded to construct novel strict kinematic algebras on various slices near null infinity.

In our search for a strict kinematic algebra, we restricted ourselves to axial-type (partial) gauge constrains in the current article. We anticipate that there are a number of other options for revealing the strict kinematic algebra, via the vanishing of the
\theta
terms in (
125
) and (
126
), which exactly parametrise the failure of the algebra to be strict. We leave these for future work.

The natural next step is to extend our results to self-dual gravity. In this framework, it would be intriguing to explore whether the fall-off behaviour of the metric components, stemming from asymptotic flatness, can similarly be derived from a homotopy algebraic perspective. Additionally, incorporating the BMS symmetry group into this analysis presents an exciting avenue for future work. The presence of the strict kinematic algebras identified in this article also suggests that the double copy will proceed straightforwardly
29
29

29

For some previous work on the double copy at null infinity, see
[
24
,
71
,
29
,
72
,
73
,
74
]
.

So far, our analysis has centred on the self-dual sub-sectors of field theories, where the cochain complex exhibits a simpler structure, and the
L_{\infty}
algebra is limited to brackets no higher than
B_{2}
. Extending this framework to encompass full Yang-Mills and gravity will require working with a more intricate structure, including the presence of higher-order brackets. Nevertheless, we anticipate that the master equation in (
104d
) will admit a natural generalization of the following form:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\Pi\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{n\leq 0}

(164a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\Pi\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\Pi B_{1})

(164b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}\left(\Pi B_{1}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi},\Pi B_{2}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}\right)=0

(164c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}\left(\Pi B_{2}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}\right)+[\Pi B_{1}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi},\Pi B_{3}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}]=0

(164d)

\displaystyle\Pi B_{2}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}\Pi B_{3}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}+\Pi B_{3}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}\Pi B_{2}|_{\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi\otimes\mathrm{Im}\,\Pi}=0\;,

(164e)

where in the second to last relation we included a term containing the three-bracket
B_{3}
that captures the quartic interactions of Yang-Mills, and the last relation schematically represents a higher homotopy compatibility relation between the two-bracket
B_{2}
and the higher bracket
B_{3}
.

The study of infrared phenomena and their relations to asymptotic symmetries has more recently been extended to include subleading effects
[
75
,
76
,
67
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
,
29
,
91
,
92
,
93
,
94
]
. This has unveiled infinite symmetry algebras
[
95
,
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
30
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
112
,
113
,
89
,
33
,
91
,
114
,
27
]
, which appear in the OPE’s of the CFT on the boundary in celestial holography. On the YM side, the relevant algebra is the so-called S-algebra (see
[
115
]
and
[
116
]
for its phase space realisation). It would be interesting to include the overleading large gauge parameters (which are known to lead to subleading effects, see e.g.
[
24
]
and
[
29
]
for SDYM) in our analysis to see if the homotopy algebra gives rise to some known deformations of the S-algebra. On the gravity side, the corresponding infinite algebra is the
w_{1+\infty}
algebra; here a potentially useful starting point is
[
30
]
, where the
w_{1+\infty}
algebra was derived from the kinematic algebra in its original presentation as an area-preserving diffeomorphism in the self-dual sector
[
6
]
. Finally, another long-term goal to explore how to use the homotopy-transfer-based framework developed in the context of AdS/CFT in
[
58
]
in this setting.

Appendix A
Construction of the master system’s solutions

In this appendix, we provide a detailed proof of the solutions (
116
) for the master system (
104d
), using the formalism introduced in
section

5.2
. We start by listing some useful properties of the projection
\mathcal{P}_{N}
, then we formulate the conditions that the power selection sets in (
109
) must satisfy in order for
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
to solve the system. Finally, we construct the infinite family of physically relevant solutions
\{\mathcal{N}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}
for these conditions, which in turn allows us to derive the
L_{\infty}
algebras encoding self-dual YM theory near null infinity.

A.1
Properties of
\mathcal{P}_{N}

In this subsection, we provide some useful properties of the projection
\mathcal{P}_{N}
defined in (
107
), which will be frequently referenced in the remainder of this appendix.

We begin by introducing some notation. Let
M,N\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. We define the complement of
M
in
\mathbb{Z}
and the addition of the sets
M
and
N
, respectively, as

M^{\complement}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}\setminus M\;,\quad M+N\coloneqq\{m+n\ |\ m\in M,n\in N\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\;.

(165)

Moreover, we adopt the following notation for the projector
\mathcal{P}_{N}
: if
N
is a singleton
\{n\}
, we simply write
\mathcal{P}_{n}:=\mathcal{P}_{\{n\}}
. With this in mind, and recalling that we work with Bondi coordinates
x^{\mu}=(r,y^{\alpha})=(r,u,z,\bar{z})
, we can prove the properties below.

Lemma A.1

(Properties of projection)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{N}
be the map

\mathcal{P}_{N}f(r,y^{\alpha}):=\sum_{n\in N}r^{n}f^{(n)}(y^{\alpha})

(
107
)

and
\mathcal{T}
the translation in (
115
). Then
\forall s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\forall L,M,N\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}_{r}(\mathcal{M})
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{M\cap N}

(166a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}-\mathcal{P}_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{M\setminus N}\quad\mathrm{if\ }N\subseteq M

(166b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}(r^{s}f)

\displaystyle=r^{s}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-s}M}f

(166c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}\partial_{r}=\mathcal{P}_{M\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}M\setminus\{0\}}=\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}M}

(166d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}\partial_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{M}

(166e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}(fg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in M}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{P}_{m-n}(f)\mathcal{P}_{n}(g)

(166f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{M}(f)\mathcal{P}_{N}(g))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}_{M}(f)\mathcal{P}_{N}(g)\quad\mathrm{if\ }L\supseteq M+N\\
0\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \ \mathrm{if\ }L\subseteq(M+N)^{\complement}\end{cases}

(166g)

In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{N}
is a projector and the commutator
[\mathcal{P}_{M},\mathcal{P}_{N}]
vanishes for all
M,N
.

Proof.

\mathcal{P}_{M}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{M}=\sum_{m\in M}\mathcal{P}_{m}\;,

(167)

where
\mathcal{P}_{m}
acts as

\mathcal{P}_{m}(f)\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{mn}r^{n}f^{(n)}=r^{m}f^{(m)}\;.

(168)

By virtue of this trivial identity, we can prove the above lemma in the simple case when
L,M
and
N
are singletons; the general result can then be recovered using (
167
).
\forall l,m,n,s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}_{r}(\mathcal{M})
the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m}\mathcal{P}_{n}f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{m}(r^{n}f^{(n)})=\delta_{mn}r^{n}f^{(n)}=\delta_{mn}\mathcal{P}_{n}f

(169a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m}(r^{s}f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{m,n+s}r^{n+s}f^{(n)}=r^{m}f^{(m-s)}=r^{s}\mathcal{P}_{m-s}f

(169b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m}\partial_{r}f

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\delta_{m,n-1}r^{n-1}f^{(n)}=(m+1)r^{m}f^{(m+1)}

(169c)

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}(r^{m+1}f^{(m+1)})=\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{m+1}f

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m}(fg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,n\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{m,l+n}r^{l+n}f^{(l)}g^{(n)}=r^{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(m-n)}g^{(n)}

(169d)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{P}_{m-n}(f)\mathcal{P}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{l}[\mathcal{P}_{m}(f)\mathcal{P}_{n}(g)]

\displaystyle=\delta_{l,m+n}r^{m+n}f^{(m)}g^{(n)}=\delta_{l,m+n}\mathcal{P}_{m}(f)\mathcal{P}_{n}(g)\;.

(169e)

The first equation above implies that
\mathcal{P}_{m}
is a projector (since
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{m}
), while from the third it is clear that

\mathcal{P}_{-1}(\partial_{r}f)=0\quad\forall f\in C^{\infty}_{r}(\mathcal{M})\ .

(170)

Note that this equation holds because we do not include logarithms in the definition of
C^{\infty}_{r}(\mathcal{M})
, see the expansion (
73
). It is easy to see that
\mathcal{P}_{m}
commutes with
\partial_{\alpha}
. Equations (
166a
)-(
166f
) follow straightforwardly. For instance:

\mathcal{P}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{N}=\sum_{m\in M}\sum_{n\in N}\mathcal{P}_{m}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\sum_{m\in M}\sum_{n\in N}\mathcal{P}_{m}\delta_{mn}=\sum_{m\in M\cap N}\mathcal{P}_{m}=\mathcal{P}_{M\cap N}\ ,

(171)

thus
\mathcal{P}_{M}
is a projector.
∎

A.2
Conditions on the power selection sets

As discussed in
section

5.2.2
, the morphism
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with power selection sets satisfying (
114c
) is the most general solution for
\Pi
for the first equation of the master system. Below, we find the additional conditions that the sets in
\mathcal{N}
must satisfy in order for
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
to solve all the other equations of the master system, too. In this way, we end up with a system of equations for the power selection sets. A projection
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with
\mathcal{N}
solving such a system will be the desired solution for
\Pi
in (
104d
).

A.2.1
Cochain map condition

We start by looking at the second equation of the master system (
104d
), the one encoding the request that
\Pi
is a cochain map.

Lemma A.2

(Cochain map condition)
.

Let
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
be the morphism defined in (
111
) with the sets in
\mathcal{N}
obeying condition (
114c
). Then
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is a solution for
\Pi
in equation (
104b
) iff the corresponding power selection sets satisfy all the inclusions
30
30

30

\mathcal{T}
is the translation defined in (
115
).

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}

(172a)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{\Lambda}

(172b)

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{r}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-2}\left(N_{A_{z}}\cap N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\right)

(172c)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{r}}

(172d)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

(172e)

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{u}}\cap N_{A_{z}}\;.

(172f)

Proof.

We want to find the constraints that the sets in
\mathcal{N}
shall satisfy in order to have
\mathrm{Ker}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1})
. Following the same approach discussed in
section

5.1
31
31

31

Cfr. equations (
94
) and (
100
).
, it is convenient to rewrite this expression as
\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1})
, where
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}:=1_{\mathcal{X}}-\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is the projection to the complement of
\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
in
\mathcal{X}
32
32

32

Recall that, by hypothesis,
\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{n\leq 0}\subset\mathcal{X}
, see equations (
92
) and (
104a
).
. This condition is equivalent to asking that

\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi):=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}(\psi)=0\quad\forall\psi\in\mathcal{X}\;,

(173)

where we introduce the notation
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}
to increase readability in what follows. From equations (
111
) and (
166b
) we find that,
\forall\psi\in\mathcal{X}
,

\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}(\psi)=\psi-\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi)=\begin{cases}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}})\Lambda=\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}^{\complement}}\Lambda&\mathrm{if}\ \psi\in X_{-1}\\
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}})A_{\mu}=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}^{\complement}}A_{\mu}&\mathrm{if}\ \psi\in X_{0}\\
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{\mu\nu}}})E_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{\mu\nu}}^{\complement}}E_{\mu\nu}&\mathrm{if}\ \psi\in X_{1}\end{cases}\;.

(174)

where
N_{I}^{\complement}:=\mathbb{Z}\setminus N_{I}
is the complement of the set
N_{I}
, with
I\in\{\Lambda,A_{\mu},E_{\mu\nu}\}
.

This observation allows us to rewrite
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi)
in terms of the projection
\mathcal{P}
, so that we can manipulate it using the properties listed in (
166
). If
\psi=\Lambda\in X_{-1}
, then
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda)
is an element of
X_{0}
(
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}
has degree
+1
, since it contains the differential) and its components are
33
33

33

Recall equations (
111
), (
78
) and (
174
) for
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
,
B_{1}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\complement}
, respectively. Moreover, use properties (
166a
), (
166d
) and (
166e
).

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda)_{r}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}^{\complement}}\Lambda=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}^{\complement}}\partial_{r}\Lambda

(175)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}^{\complement}}\Lambda=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}\cap N_{\Lambda}^{\complement}}\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda\;.

To study when these expressions reduce to zero, note that given any subsets
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, the intersection
U\cap V^{\complement}
vanishes iff
U\subseteq V
. Moreover, the translation
\mathcal{T}_{s}U
of the set
U
by an arbitrary integer
s
satisfies the relation
(\mathcal{T}_{s}U)^{\complement}=\mathcal{T}_{s}(U^{\complement})
. Thus, the components in (
175
) vanish
\forall\Lambda\in X_{-1}
iff the inclusions (
172a
) and (
172b
) are satisfied. A similar argument applies when
\psi=A_{\mu}\in X_{0}
. In this case,
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)
is an equation of motion in
X_{1}
and its independent components are given by

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{ru}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big[\partial_{[r}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A}^{\complement}}A\big)_{u]}+r^{-2}\partial_{[z}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A}^{\complement}}A\big)_{\bar{z}]}\big]

(176)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}^{\complement}}\partial_{r}A_{u}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{\complement}}\partial_{u}A_{r}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-2}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{\complement}}(r^{-2}\partial_{z}A_{\bar{z}})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-2}N_{A_{z}}^{\complement}}(r^{-2}\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{z})\big]

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{r\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=4\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\partial_{[r}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A}^{\complement}}A\big)_{\bar{z}]}=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{\complement}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}A_{\bar{z}}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{\complement}}\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{r}\big)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{uz}

\displaystyle=4\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}}\partial_{[u}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A}^{\complement}}A\big)_{z]}=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{z}}^{\complement}}\partial_{u}A_{z}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{u}}^{\complement}}\partial_{z}A_{u}\big)\;.

Since we want these expressions to vanish for arbitrary
A_{\mu}
, we require that each term above vanishes independently (because different terms in a given component of
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{\mu\nu}
depend on different components of
A_{\mu}
). For instance,

\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{uz}=0\quad\forall A_{\mu}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}\cap N_{A_{z}}^{\complement}}\partial_{u}A_{z}=0&\forall A_{z}\\
\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}\cap N_{A_{u}}^{\complement}}\partial_{z}A_{u}=0&\forall A_{u}\end{cases}\ \Leftrightarrow\ N_{E_{uz}}\subseteq N_{A_{u}}\cap N_{A_{z}}\;,

(177)

which gives equation (
172f
). Similarly, one can prove that
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{ru}
and
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(A)_{r\bar{z}}
vanish for all
A_{\mu}
iff equations (
172c
), (
172d
) and (
172e
) hold. Finally, by degree,
\Psi_{\mathcal{N}}(E)=0
for all
E\in X_{1}
, so no additional information on
\mathcal{N}
can be extracted from this condition.
∎

A.2.2
Leibniz rule condition

The next step is to determine the additional conditions that the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}
must satisfy to ensure that
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is a solution of the Leibniz rule in (
104c
).

Lemma A.3

(Leibniz rule condition)
.

Let
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
be the morphism defined in (
111
) with the sets in
\mathcal{N}
obeying conditions (
114c
) and (
172
). Then
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is a solution for
\Pi
in equation (
104c
) iff the corresponding power selection sets satisfy all the following equations:
34
34

34

As a reminder, we reference the notation defined in (
165
).

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\big[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\big]

\displaystyle=\emptyset

(178a)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\alpha}}\cap\big(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\alpha}}+N_{\Lambda}\big)

\displaystyle=\emptyset

(178b)

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{r}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{0\})\cup\mathcal{T}_{-2}(N_{A_{z}}\cup N_{A_{\bar{z}}})\big)\setminus N_{E_{ru}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]

\displaystyle=\emptyset

(178c)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{r}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus\{0\})\big)\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]

\displaystyle=\emptyset

(178d)

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{u}}\cup N_{A_{z}}\big)\setminus N_{E_{uz}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]

\displaystyle=\emptyset\;,

(178e)

together with the conditions

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}\ni-1\

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ N_{\Lambda}\ni 0

(179a)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\ni-1\

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\ni 0\;.

(179b)

Proof.

The goal is to determine the conditions on the sets in the collection
\mathcal{N}
so that

\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=0\quad\forall\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\;,

(180)

where, for brevity, we defined
\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}:=\mathrm{Leib}\left(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}|_{\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}},\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}|_{\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\otimes\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}}\right)
. By degree, the above equation is a trivial identity when the sum of the degree of
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
is non-negative. Moreover, the Leibniz rule is graded symmetric
35
35

35

From equations (
29
) and (
31
) it follows that
\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=(-1)^{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}}\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\psi_{2},\psi_{1})
.
. Therefore, the problem reduces to the study of the two cases with
\psi_{1}\in X_{-1}
and
\psi_{2}
belonging to either
X_{-1}
or
X_{0}
.

To begin, we apply
\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}
to two gauge parameters
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\cap X_{-1}
, obtaining the following gauge field:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})_{\mu}+\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda_{1})_{\mu},\Lambda_{2})-\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda_{1},\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda_{2})_{\mu})

(181)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}}\big(\partial_{\mu}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda_{1})_{\mu},\Lambda_{2}]+[\Lambda_{1},\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda_{2})_{\mu}]\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}}\big(-\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]+2[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}}\partial_{\mu}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]\big)\;,

where we used the expressions (
78
) and (
79
) for
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, respectively, and the definition of
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
. At this point, it is worth noting the following fact: since
\Lambda_{i}
(with
i=1,2
) is an element of
\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
is idempotent, it is always possible to write

\Lambda_{i}=\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\Lambda_{i}\;.

(182)

By applying this observation, together with the properties in (
166
), the
r
-component of the expression above takes the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})_{r}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]+2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]

(183)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}}[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]

\displaystyle+2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]\;.

The projector
\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}
in the last line can be written as
\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}+\delta_{A_{r},-1}\mathcal{P}_{-1}
, where

\delta_{A_{r},-1}:=\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{if}\ N_{A_{r}}\ni-1\\
0&\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(184)

Moreover, equation (
172a
) (which holds by hypothesis) implies
N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}=N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}
. Thus the above expression becomes

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})_{r}

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}[(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus\{-1\}}-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}})\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]

(185)

\displaystyle+2\delta_{A_{r},-1}\mathcal{P}_{-1}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]

\displaystyle

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\Lambda_{2]}]

\displaystyle+2\delta_{A_{r},-1}\mathcal{P}_{-1}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda_{[1},\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\Lambda_{2]}]\;,

where in the second equality we used (
166b
). The last line vanishes for all
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. We prove this in two steps. First, observe that the hypotheses of this lemma imply that:

N_{A_{r}}\not\ni 0\;.

(186)

To show why this is true, we argue by contradiction. Suppose
N_{A_{r}}\ni 0
, then from (
172a
) we conclude that
1\in N_{\Lambda}
. However, this contradicts (
114a
), since all the sets we are considering here must not contain positive integers. Thus,
N_{A_{r}}\not\ni 0
. The second step is to note that
\max N_{\Lambda}\leq 0
and
\max N_{A_{r}}<0
, thanks to (
114a
) and (
186
), hence
36
36

36

Here we use the fact that for all sets
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, the maximum of the sum of the two sets is the sum of their maxima, i.e.
\max(U+V)=\max U+\max V
.

\max(N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{\Lambda})<-1\ \Rightarrow\ -1\not\in N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{\Lambda}\;,

(187)

which implies that the second term in the RHS of (
185
) is zero by virtue of (
166g
). Employing this property once more, we observe that the other term vanishes for all
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
iff equation (
178a
) is satisfied. Now, consider the other components of (
181
), namely
\mu=\alpha
. By following the same line of reasoning as above, we find
37
37

37

Use properties (
166a
), (
166b
), (
166e
), (
166g
), (
172b
) and equation (
182
).

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}\big(-\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]+2[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]\big)

(188)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}\cap N_{\Lambda}}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]+2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda_{[1},\Lambda_{2]}]

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\alpha}}}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\alpha}}}\partial_{\alpha}\Lambda_{[1},\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\Lambda_{2]}]\ ,

that is zero for all
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\cap X_{-1}
iff
N_{\Lambda}
and
N_{A_{\alpha}}
satisfy equation (
178b
).

We now turn to the case where we apply the Leibniz rule to a gauge parameter
\Lambda\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\cap X_{-1}
and a gauge field
A_{\mu}\in\mathrm{Im}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}\cap X_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda,A)_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda,A)_{\mu\nu}+\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda),A)_{\mu\nu}-\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda,\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(A))_{\mu\nu}\;.

(189)

The line of reasoning is the same as before, although the expressions are more involved. Let us analyse the three independent components of (
189
) one by one, starting from the
ru
-component:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda,A)_{ru}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big(\partial_{[r}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda,A)_{u]}+r^{-2}\partial_{[z}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{2}(\Lambda,A)_{\bar{z}]}+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda)_{[r},A_{u]}]

(190)

\displaystyle+r^{-2}[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(\Lambda)_{[z},A_{\bar{z}]}]+\tfrac{1}{2}[\Lambda,\pi_{\mathcal{N}}B_{1}(A)_{ru}]\big)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big(-\partial_{[r}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}([\Lambda,A])_{u]}-r^{-2}\partial_{[z}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}([\Lambda,A])_{\bar{z}]}+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[r},A_{u]}]

\displaystyle+r^{-2}[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[z},A_{\bar{z}]}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}(\partial_{[r}A_{u]}+r^{-2}\partial_{[z}A_{\bar{z}]})]\big)\;.

Let
T_{1,ru}
be the sum of the terms above that contain
A_{r}
and
A_{u}
. At this point, we apply the same trick as before: we take advantage of the identities
\Lambda=\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\Lambda
and
A_{\mu}=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\mu}}}A_{\mu}
, together with the equations in (
166
), to find

\displaystyle T_{1,ru}

\displaystyle:=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\left(-\partial_{[r}\pi_{N}([\Lambda,A])_{u]}+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[r},A_{u]}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\partial_{[r}A_{u]}]\right)

(191)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big\{\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}\left([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{r}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}\partial_{u}A_{r}]\right)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{-1\}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{u}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}A_{u}]\big)

\displaystyle+[\mathcal{P}_{N(A_{r})\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{u}]-[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{u}}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{r}]+2[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\partial_{[r}A_{u]}]\big\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{r}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}}\partial_{u}A_{r}]-[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{u}]

\displaystyle-[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}A_{u}]+[\mathcal{P}_{N(A_{r})\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{u}]-[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{u}}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{r}]

\displaystyle+2[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\partial_{[r}A_{u]}]\big)\ ,

where in the last equality we make use of (
172a
)-(
172c
) to simplify the projectors
38
38

38

In particular, equation (
172c
) implies that
N_{E_{ru}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{-1\}
, since from (
114a
) together with (
114c
) it follows that
N_{E_{ru}}\not\ni-1
.
. Employing property (
166b
), we obtain

\displaystyle T_{1,ru}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{r}]-[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{u}]

(192)

\displaystyle+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{ru}}}\partial_{u}A_{r}]-[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus(N_{E_{ru}}\cup\{-1\})}\partial_{r}A_{u}]\big)\ .

Let us focus on the first term on the RHS. We want to analyse it using the properties of the projector
\mathcal{P}
. To this end, define the set

S:=N_{E_{ru}}\cap\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{A_{r}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}\;.

(193)

By virtue of the second line in equation (
166g
), we know that the first term in the RHS of (
192
) vanishes for all
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
when the set
S
above is empty. To determine when
S=\emptyset
, consider the following key observations.

i)

From equations (
172c
) and (
178b
) we deduce that

N_{E_{ru}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\;.

(194)

ii)

Equation (
172a
) implies

N_{A_{r}}=\begin{cases}N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cup\{-1\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\cup\{-1\}&\mathrm{if}\ N_{A_{r}}\ni-1\\
N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}&\mathrm{if}\ N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1\;.\end{cases}

(195)

iii)

Let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. It is always true that

\mathcal{T}_{s}(U+V)=\mathcal{T}_{s}U+V=U+\mathcal{T}_{s}V\;.

(196)

We emphasize that this identity means that the addition of sets of integers defined in (
165
) and the usual notion of union of sets behave differently with the translation (
115
)
39
39

39

Recall that
\mathcal{T}_{s}(U\cup V)=\mathcal{T}_{s}U\cup\mathcal{T}_{s}V
.
. Moreover, the identity element for the addition of sets is the singleton containing the identity element of the addition in
\mathbb{Z}
, that is to say
U+\{0\}=\{0\}+U=U
for all
U
. Together, these two facts suggest a useful relation:

\displaystyle U+(\mathcal{T}_{-1}V\cup\{-1\})=\mathcal{T}_{-1}(U+(V\cup\{0\}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{-1}[(U+V)\cup U]\;.

(197)

We combine equation (
193
) with (
194
)-(
197
) and we distinguish two different cases:

1.

if
N_{A_{r}}\ni-1
, then

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda}\right)^{\complement}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}\left[(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})\cup N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}\right]

(198)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{-1}\big\{[(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\cap(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})]

\displaystyle\cup[(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\cap N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}]\big\}=\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad N_{\Lambda}\ni 0\;,

where we used the fact that for
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}
we have

(U+V)^{\complement}\cap U=\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad U\subseteq U+V\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad V\ni 0\;.

(199)

2.

If
N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1
, then

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda}\right)^{\complement}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\;.

(200)

Hence, we find that the first term in (
192
) vanishes
\forall\Lambda,A_{\mu}
if we impose condition (
179a
), which comes from the first case above. The second term in (
192
) is zero as well, as one can see by employing a similar argument, which results in the following chain of inclusions:

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}

\displaystyle\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{A_{u}}\right]

(201)

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]^{\complement}\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]=\emptyset\;.

Conversely, the two terms in the second line of (
192
) do not vanish for arbitrary
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
unless

\begin{cases}N_{E_{ru}}\cap(N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{ru}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\\
N_{E_{ru}}\cap\big[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\setminus(N_{E_{ru}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset\;.\end{cases}

(202)

At this point, we focus on the remaining terms in (
190
), denoting their sum as
T_{2,ru}
. After some algebra (completely analogous to what we do above for
T_{1,ru}
, with the only difference that now there is an additional coefficient
r^{-2}
that needs to be treated carefully, via the identity (
166c
)), we rearrange the terms in
T_{2,ru}
as

\displaystyle T_{2,ru}

\displaystyle:=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}\big(-r^{-2}\partial_{[z}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}([\Lambda,A])_{\bar{z}]}+r^{-2}[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[z},A_{\bar{z}]}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}}(r^{-2}\partial_{[z}A_{\bar{z}]})]\big)

(203)

\displaystyle=-r^{-2}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{z}}}\partial_{z}\Lambda,A_{\bar{z}}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}}\partial_{z}A_{\bar{z}}]\big)-(z\leftrightarrow\bar{z})\;.

One can prove that the first term is zero by means of the following sequence of inclusions

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}\cap\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{z}}+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{z}}\cap(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{z}}+N_{\Lambda})

(204)

\displaystyle\subseteq(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{z}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\cap(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{z}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\;,

whereas the second term vanishes iff

\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}\cap(N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\;.

(205)

Analogous results hold for the last two terms in
T_{2,ru}
(the ones with
z\leftrightarrow\bar{z}
). By putting (
202
) and (
205
) together, and using (
196
), one obtains equation (
178c
).

The proof of the last two equations of this lemma is conceptually analogous to this last one. The
r\bar{z}
-component of the Leibniz rule (
189
) is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda,A)_{r\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=4\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\big(-\partial_{[r}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}([\Lambda,A])_{\bar{z}]}+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[r},A_{\bar{z}]}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\partial_{[r}A_{\bar{z}]}]\big)\;.

(206)

This expression can be expanded using (
111
), obtaining a sum of many terms. We find it useful to group them as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{1,r\bar{z}}&:=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\Lambda,A_{r}]-[\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{\bar{z}}]\big)\\
&\quad+2\delta_{E_{r\bar{z}},-1}\mathcal{P}_{-1}\big([\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{r}]+[\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}\Lambda,A_{\bar{z}}]\\
&\quad+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}}\partial_{r}A_{\bar{z}}]\big)\end{split}

(207a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{2,r\bar{z}}&:=2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}}\big([\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}}\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{r}]-[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus(N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\cup\{-1\})}\partial_{r}A_{\bar{z}}]\big)\\
&\quad+2\delta_{E_{r\bar{z}},-1}\mathcal{P}_{-1}[\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\Lambda,A_{r}]\;,\end{split}

(207b)

where
\delta_{E_{r\bar{z}},-1}\coloneqq 1
if
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\ni-1
and it vanishes otherwise. Once again, we use a sequence of inclusions and equation (
166g
) to prove that
T_{1,r\bar{z}}=0
for all
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
. Let us start by focusing on the first term of (
207a
). We distinguish two cases:

1.

if
N_{A_{r}}\ni-1
, then
40
40

40

Use equations (
172e
), (
172a
), (
197
), (
178b
), (
199
) and (
179a
), in this order.

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\setminus\{-1\}\cap(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{A_{r}})

(208)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\cap\left[N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+(\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\cup\{-1\})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}[(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})\cup N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}\big\{(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\cap[(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})\cup N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}]\big\}=\emptyset\;.

The last line above is equal to the empty set since we require condition (
179a
) to hold (the steps to prove this closely mirror those in (
198
)).

2.

If
N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1
, then

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\setminus\{-1\}\cap(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{A_{r}})

(209)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\;.

In both cases, the first term of
T_{1,r\bar{z}}
vanishes. The second term in (
207a
) is zero as well, thanks to the following chain of inclusions:

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\setminus\{-1\}\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\right]

(210)

\displaystyle\subseteq N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]^{\complement}\cap\left[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\right]=\emptyset\;.

We observe that the other terms in
T_{1,r\bar{z}}
vanish when
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\not\ni-1
, because of the overall factor
\delta_{E_{r\bar{z}},-1}
. Hence, suppose
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\ni-1
. Then, from equations (
172d
) and (
179a
) we deduce that
N_{A_{r}}\ni-1
and
N_{\Lambda}\ni 0
. Moreover,
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}
cannot contain zero
41
41

41

N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}
is contained in
N_{A_{r}}
by hypothesis, see (
172d
), so (
186
) implies that
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\not\ni 0
.
. Thus,

\displaystyle\max N_{\Lambda}=0\;,\quad\max N_{A_{r}}=\max N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}=-1\;,

(211)

which implies that

\displaystyle\max\left(N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda}\right)<-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ -1\not\in N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda}

(212)

\displaystyle\max\left(N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\right)<-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ -1\not\in N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\max\left(N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{\Lambda}\right)<-1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ -1\not\in N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}+N_{\Lambda}\;.

This means that each term in the second and third line of (
207a
) vanish
42
42

42

By virtue of (
166g
).
. Therefore, we conclude that
T_{1,r\bar{z}}=0
for all
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
, and it does not provide any additional conditions on the power selection sets. Conversely,
T_{2,r\bar{z}}=0
for all
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
iff

\begin{cases}N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap(N_{A_{r}}\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda})=\emptyset\\
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus(N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}]=\emptyset\\
\max(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}+N_{A_{r}})<-1\;.\end{cases}

(213)

The first two conditions are equivalent to (
178d
). Given (
211
), we deduce that the third equation in the system above is true only when
N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\ni 0
, so that the maximum of the set
N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\bar{z}}}
is strictly less than zero. In other words, the third equation in (
213
) corresponds to condition (
179b
). This concludes the study of the
r\bar{z}
-component of the Leibniz rule.

Finally, the
uz
-component of (
189
) is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda,A)_{uz}

\displaystyle=4\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}}\big(-\partial_{[u}\pi_{\mathcal{N}}([\Lambda,A])_{z]}+[\pi_{\mathcal{N}}(\partial\Lambda)_{[u},A_{z]}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}}\partial_{[u}A_{z]}]\big)

(214)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}}\big([\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}}\partial_{u}\Lambda,A_{z}]+[\Lambda,\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{z}}\setminus N_{E_{uz}}}\partial_{u}A_{z}]\big)-(u\leftrightarrow z)\;.

By applying one last time the procedure described multiple times in this proof, one can show that the first term above is always zero thanks to the inclusion

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}\cap\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{A_{z}}\right)\subseteq N_{A_{u}}\cap\left(N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{u}}+N_{\Lambda}\right)=\emptyset\ ,

(215)

while the necessary and sufficient condition for the second term in (
214
) to vanish is
N_{E_{uz}}\subseteq(N_{A_{u}}\setminus N_{E_{uz}}+N_{\Lambda})^{\complement}
. Similar considerations hold for the other two terms in (
214
), with
u\leftrightarrow z
. Thus, with the hypotheses of this lemma,
\mathrm{Leib}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Lambda,A)_{uz}
vanishes for all
\Lambda,A_{\mu}
iff the power selection sets satisfy equation (
178e
).
∎

A.2.3
Physically relevant solutions

Let us highlight that the two lemmas above hold for arbitrary sets
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}
, with
I\in\{\Lambda,A_{\mu},E_{\mu\nu}\}
, since in none of the steps of their proofs did we rely on the explicit form of these sets. In particular,
N_{I}
may contain gaps, or they may only consist of negative numbers far from zero. In such cases, however, the power selection sets describe a physically non-interesting situation, since the presence of gaps in the powers of the
1/r
expansion and/or the absence of the leading powers (the ones closer to zero) mean that the hierarchy of subleading terms is not respected. To eliminate these scenarios and extract the physical solution from the master system, we shall introduce two additional (and quite natural) assumptions on the power selection sets:

i)

N_{I}
are discrete intervals (i.e. each set contains consecutive integers without gaps). For convenience, we introduce here the following notation: for any two integers
a\leq b
, we define the discrete interval

\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\coloneqq[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}=\{a,\dots,b\}\;,

(216)

where
[a,b]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an interval in the usual sense. With this notation, the assumption we are making can be expressed as follows: the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}
are of the form
N_{I}=\llbracket a_{I},b_{I}\rrbracket
for some integers
a_{I}\leq b_{I}
and for all
I
.

ii)

We assume that each power selection set
N_{I}
contains the maximum integer allowed, where by “allowed” we mean compatible with (
114c
) and with the constraints given by lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
. A more formal way to state this assumption is the following: for each
I
, define

\mathfrak{N}_{I}^{\mathrm{ms}}\coloneqq\{N_{I}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\ \mathrm{satisfying\ \eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N},\eqref{eq:conditions on N from commuting diagram},\eqref{eq:conditions on N from Leibniz}\ and\ \eqref{eq:conditions on N from Leibniz last ones}}\}\;.

(217)

In other words,
\mathfrak{N}_{I}^{\mathrm{ms}}
is the space of solutions of the first three equations of the master system for the power selection set
N_{I}
. Then, we shall restrict to the subspace

\tilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{I}^{\mathrm{ms}}\coloneqq\Big\{\tilde{N}_{I}\in\mathfrak{N}_{I}^{\mathrm{ms}}\ \Big|\ \max\tilde{N}_{I}=\max_{N_{I}\in\mathfrak{N}_{I}}\left(\max N_{I}\right)\Big\}\;.

(218)

To sum up, building on the two assumptions above, we state that the physically relevant solutions of the first three equations of the master system are given by projectors
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with power selection sets
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}
of the form

N_{I}=\llbracket a_{I},b_{I}\rrbracket\in\tilde{\mathfrak{N}}_{I}^{\mathrm{ms}}\;,

(219)

for some non-positive integers
a_{I}\leq b_{I}
and for all
I\in\{\Lambda,A_{\mu},E_{\mu\nu}\}
.
This means that each power selection set in the collection
\mathcal{N}
is a discrete interval whose maximum is as close to zero as possible, consistent with (
114c
) and the constraints given by lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
.

A.2.4
Master system for
\mathcal{N}

One last condition in the master system needs to be examined: the Jacobiator of the two-bracket, equation (
104d
). It turns out that a morphism
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with power selection sets of the form (
219
)
43
43

43

We emphasize that (
219
) implies, by definition, that
N_{I}
satisfies (
114c
), (
172
), (
178
) and (
179
)
\forall I
.
solves the Jacobiator equation for
\Pi
automatically, without the need to impose further constraints on
\mathcal{N}
. This can be proven by following a procedure that is fully analogous to the proofs of lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
, although considerably longer and more intricate. A different strategy to show this result is to check that the projections
\pi_{\mathcal{N}_{k}}
in (
116
) (which, as we will see shortly, are the solutions of the first three equations of the master system) solve the Jacobiator, too. One can do that explicitly for arbitrary values of
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}
by employing the Mathematica package developed by one of the authors, to be presented in
[
117
]
.

To conclude, in this subsection we proved that the most general solution for the first three equations of the master system (
104d
) is a projection of the form
\Pi=\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with the sets in the collection
\mathcal{N}
satisfying all the relations contained in (
114c
), (
172
), (
178
) and (
179
). For brevity, we call this set of
16
equations the
master system for
\mathcal{N}
. We reproduce it here for convenience:

\displaystyle N_{I}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}\quad\forall I\in\{\Lambda,A_{\mu},E_{\mu\nu}\}

(
114a
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{\mu\nu}}=N_{E_{\nu\mu}}

(
114b
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{z\bar{z}}}=\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}

(
114c
)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\mu}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{-\delta_{r\mu}}N_{\Lambda}

(
172a
,
172b
,
179a
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}\subseteq N_{A_{r}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{u}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-2}\left(N_{A_{z}}\cap N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\right)

(
172c
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\subseteq N_{A_{r}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

(
172d
,
172e
,
179b
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}\subseteq N_{A_{u}}\cap N_{A_{z}}

(
172f
)

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\big[\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{\Lambda}\setminus(N_{A_{r}}\cup\{-1\})+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset

(
178a
)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\alpha}}\cap\big[N_{\Lambda}\setminus N_{A_{\alpha}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset

(
178b
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{r}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{0\})\cup\mathcal{T}_{-2}(N_{A_{z}}\cup N_{A_{\bar{z}}})\big)\setminus N_{E_{ru}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset

(
178c
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\setminus\{-1\}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{r}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\setminus\{0\})\big)\setminus N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset

(
178d
)

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}\cap\big[\big(N_{A_{u}}\cup N_{A_{z}}\big)\setminus N_{E_{uz}}+N_{\Lambda}\big]=\emptyset\;.

(
178e
)

Moreover, we showed how to impose additional natural conditions on the power selection sets to ensure that the corresponding solutions of the master system for
\mathcal{N}
are physically relevant. In the next paragraph, we show how to construct a family
\{\mathcal{N}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}
of such solutions.

A.3
Solutions of the master system for
\mathcal{N}

In this subsection, thanks to the observations above, we are finally able to provide the proof of the main result of
section

5
, that is to say equations (
116
) and (
117
).

Let us recap the situation up to this point. The master system (
104d
) is solved by a projector
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}
with power selection sets
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}
satisfying the master system for
\mathcal{N}
above. Moreover, we know that the physically interesting solutions of this system are sets
N_{I}
as in equation (
219
). In particular,
N_{I}
are discrete intervals, so in order to determine them it is sufficient to find their extrema. Equation (
217
) tells us that the maximum of each interval
N_{I}
must be the greatest possible number compatible with the system above.

The following result not only allows us to find
\max N_{I}
for every
I
, but also provides a prescription to construct the solution of the master system for
\mathcal{N}
.

Lemma A.4

(Fall-off)
.

Let
\mathcal{N}=\{N_{\Lambda},N_{A_{\mu}},N_{E_{\mu\nu}}\}
be a collection of power selection sets obeying condition (
219
). Then

N_{A_{r}}\cup N_{E_{ru}}\cup N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\not\ni-1,0\;.

(221)

Proof.

By hypothesis
44
44

44

See equations (
217
)-(
219
).
, the sets
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}
are discrete intervals satisfying the master system for
\mathcal{N}
45
45

45

Recall that the master system for
\mathcal{N}
consists of equations (
114c
), (
172
), (
178
) and (
179
).
. In particular, as discussed in the proof of
lemma

A.3
, we know that

\displaystyle\eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N 1},\eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N 3}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow N_{E_{ru}}\not\ni-1,0

(222)

\displaystyle\eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N 1},\eqref{eq:condition on N 1},\eqref{eq:condition on N 4}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow N_{A_{r}}\cup N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\not\ni 0\;,

see, for example, the line of reasoning below equations (
186
). Thus, what is left to prove is
N_{A_{r}}\cup N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\not\ni-1
. Notice that if
N_{A_{r}}
does not contain
-1
,
N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}
does not contain it either, by virtue of (
172d
).

Demonstrating that
N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1
requires additional steps. We argue by contradiction: suppose
N_{A_{r}}
does contain
-1
. We then show that the following collection of power selection sets

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}\coloneqq\{N_{\Lambda},N_{A_{\mu}},N_{E_{\mu\nu}}\ |\ N_{I}\ \mathrm{are\ physical\ solutions\ of\ \eqref{def:pi_N_contraints_on_N},\eqref{eq:conditions on N from commuting diagram},\eqref{eq:conditions on N from Leibniz last ones}}\}

(223)

fails to satisfy the Leibniz rule condition (
178
). Consequently, if
N_{A_{r}}\ni-1
there is no solution for the master system for
\mathcal{N}
.

The first step of this proof by contradiction is to understand the structure of the sets
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
. As above, the term “physical” in the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
means that

N_{I}=\llbracket\min N_{I},\max N_{I}\rrbracket

(224)

are discrete intervals with maxima as close to zero as possible. Moreover, by definition,
N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
satisfy the no divergences condition (
114c
) and the cochain map condition (
172
). In particular, they cannot contain positive integers and equation (
222
) holds. This allows us to determine the maxima of all the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
:

\max N_{I}=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\ }I=\Lambda,A_{\alpha},E_{uz}\\
-1&\mathrm{if\ }I=A_{r},E_{r\bar{z}}\\
-2&\mathrm{if\ }I=E_{ru}\;.\end{cases}

(225)

Now, observe that equations (
172c
)-(
172f
) and (
179b
) can be rearranged as follows:

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle\supseteq N_{E_{ru}}\cup N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

(226)

\displaystyle N_{A_{u}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{1}N_{E_{ru}}\cup N_{E_{uz}}

\displaystyle N_{A_{z}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}\cup N_{E_{uz}}

\displaystyle N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{1}N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}\;,

while equations (
172a
), (
172b
) and (
179a
) can be rewritten as

N_{\Lambda}\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{1}N_{A_{r}}\cup N_{A_{u}}\cup N_{A_{z}}\cup N_{A_{\bar{z}}}\;.

(227)

In the list of inclusions above, the “innermost” sets are the intervals
N_{E_{\mu\nu}}
. This means that these intervals are not constrained by (
172
) and (
179
), but only by the system (
114c
), which fixes their maxima. Therefore, the sets
N_{E_{\mu\nu}}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
are of the form

N_{E_{ru}}=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\,,\quad N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}=\llbracket n,-1\rrbracket\,,\quad N_{E_{uz}}=\llbracket l,0\rrbracket

(228)

for some integers
m\leq-2
,
n\leq-1
and
l\leq 0
. This is the starting point to find the other intervals in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
. Indeed, using (
226
) we find

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m,n),-1\rrbracket

(229)

\displaystyle N_{A_{u}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+1,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{z}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+2,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+2,n+1),0\rrbracket\;.

On the one hand, notice that the maxima of the intervals in the RHS of the inclusions above coincide with the values found in (
225
). On the other hand, we do not have any further constraints on the minima. For example, the set
N_{A_{r}}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
is of the form

N_{A_{r}}=\llbracket\min N_{A_{r}},-1\rrbracket\,,\quad\min N_{A_{r}}\leq\min(m,n)\;.

(230)

Thus, from (
227
) we obtain

N_{\Lambda}\supseteq\llbracket\min_{\mu}\left(\min N_{A_{\mu}}+\delta_{\mu r}\right),0\rrbracket\;.

(231)

Nonetheless, for simplicity, let us restrict to the case where all the inclusions in (
229
) and (
231
) reduce to equalities:

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m,n),-1\rrbracket

(232)

\displaystyle N_{A_{u}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m+1,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{z}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m+2,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m+2,n+1),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m+1,n+1,l),0\rrbracket\;.

One can prove that all the steps that follow in this proof remain valid even in the general case. Therefore, the restriction we are assuming here does not affect the generality or validity of our proof.

N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
are given by (

) and (

). A visual representation of such intervals is provided in

One can verify that they satisfy both equations (

) and (

), as we shall prove below. Let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{lhs}

\displaystyle\coloneqq N_{E_{ru}}\cap\big(J+N_{\Lambda}\big)=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\cap\big(J+\llbracket\min(m+1,n+1,l),0\rrbracket\big)\;,

(233)

where we defined

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(N_{A_{r}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{-1}(N_{A_{u}}\setminus\{0\})\cup\mathcal{T}_{-2}(N_{A_{z}}\cup N_{A_{\bar{z}}})\big)\setminus N_{E_{ru}}

(234)

\displaystyle=\big(\llbracket\min(m,n),-1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\min(m,l-1),-2\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\min(m,l-2),-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle\cup\llbracket\min(m,n-1),-2\rrbracket\big)\setminus\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\min(m,n-1,l-2),-1\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\{-1\}\cup\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mathrm{if}\ \min(m,n-1,l-2)=m\\
\llbracket\min(n-1,l-2),m-1\rrbracket&\mathrm{otherwise}\;.\end{cases}

Notice that, when
\min(m,n-1,l-2)\neq m
, one can prove that

\min(n-1,l-2)+\min(m+1,n+1,l)=2\min(n,l-1)\;.

(235)

Using this identity, we rewrite (
233
) as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{lhs}

\displaystyle=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\cap\left(\llbracket\min(m,n,l-1),-1\rrbracket\cup\begin{cases}\emptyset\\
\llbracket 2\min(n,l-1),m-1\rrbracket\end{cases}\right)

(236)

\displaystyle=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\min(m,n,l-1),-1\rrbracket=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\;,

where the two cases in the first line correspond to the same conditions of the cases in (
234
). We have established that
\mathfrak{lhs}\neq\emptyset
, which implies that equation (
178c
) is violated. Thus, we have reached the anticipated contradiction. Consequently, we conclude that our initial hypothesis is false:
N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1
.

As a consistency check, one can show that the sets in the collection

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sol}}\coloneqq\{N_{I}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}\ |\ N_{A_{r}}\not\ni-1\}

(237)

do satisfy the Leibniz rule condition (

), thus providing the most general physically relevant solution of the master system for
\mathcal{N}
. By repeating the construction outlined above, we find that

\displaystyle N_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min_{\mu}\left(\min N_{A_{\mu}}+\delta_{\mu r}\right),0\rrbracket

(238)

\displaystyle N_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m,n),-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{u}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+1,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{z}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+2,l),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{A_{\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\llbracket\min(m+2,n+1),0\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{E_{ru}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket n,-2\rrbracket

\displaystyle N_{E_{uz}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket l,0\rrbracket

for some integers

and
l\leq 0
. They differ from the sets in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{test}}
only in that

(recall that the second equality comes from (

)). Using the same reasoning as before, one can verify that the intervals in (

) solve all the inclusions in (

178c
)
46
46

46

In the simplified case where we take all the inclusions in (

reduces to

\displaystyle\mathfrak{lhs}=\llbracket m,-2\rrbracket\cap\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mathrm{if}\ \min(m,n-1,l-2)=m\\
\llbracket 2\min(n,l-1),m-1\rrbracket&\mathrm{otherwise}\par\end{cases}=\emptyset\;.

(239)

This completes the proof of (

).
∎

With

Remark B.1

(Projected chain complex)
.

Let
\Pi_{k}
with
k\leq 0
be the projection in (
116
), and let
\mathcal{K}^{(k)}
be the graded vector space resulting from the color-stripping of
\mathcal{X}^{(k)}
in (
118
). Moreover, define
b^{(k)}\coloneqq\Pi_{k}b
, where

is the differential in (
83
)
48
48

48

See

for a clarification regarding
\Pi_{k}
acting on the color-stripped space.
. Then
(\mathcal{K}^{(k)},b^{(k)})
is a chain complex.

Proof.

This is equivalent to show that

is a nilpotent map of degree
-1
. Since the projection
\Pi_{k}

(that is
-1

is nilpotent. To begin with, we write the explicit action of

\displaystyle b(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=2\left(\partial_{(r}\mathcal{A}_{u)}-r^{-2}\partial_{(z}\mathcal{A}_{\bar{z})}+r^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{u}\right)\in K_{0}

(240)

\displaystyle b(\mathcal{E})_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}r^{-2}\partial_{z}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}-(\partial_{r}+2r^{-1})\mathcal{E}_{ru}\\
r^{-2}\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathcal{E}_{uz}+\partial_{u}\mathcal{E}_{ru}\\

From the definition of

\displaystyle b^{(k)}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{A})+2\mathcal{P}_{-1}(r^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{u})\in K_{0}^{(k)}

(242)

\displaystyle b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{r}\\
\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{u}\\
\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k,-2\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{z}\\
\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{\bar{z}}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{1}^{(k)}\;,

(243)

where now
\mathcal{A}\in K_{1}^{(k)}
and

. For instance, the

results from

\begin{split}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{r}&=(\Pi_{k}b(\mathcal{E}))_{r}=(\Pi_{k}b(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{E}))_{r}\\
&=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{k}}\Big(r^{-2}\partial_{z}\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}^{k}}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}-(\partial_{r}+2r^{-1})\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}^{k}}\mathcal{E}_{ru}\Big)\\
&=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{k}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-2}N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}^{k}}(r^{-2}\partial_{z}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}})-\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{k}\cap\mathcal{T}_{-1}N_{E_{ru}}^{k}}[(\partial_{r}+2r^{-1})\mathcal{E}_{ru}]\\
&=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-4\rrbracket}(r^{-2}\partial_{z}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}})-\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}[(\partial_{r}+2r^{-1})\mathcal{E}_{ru}]\\
&=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{r}-r^{-2}\partial_{z}\cancel{\mathcal{P}_{-1}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}}\;,\end{split}

(244)

where the second term in the last line vanishes
49
49

49

Indeed,
\mathcal{P}_{-1}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}=\mathcal{P}_{\{-1\}\cap\llbracket k-2,-2\rrbracket}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}=0
.
. Clearly,
b^{(k)}(b^{(k)}(u))
vanishes for all

, by degree. Using (

)-(

\begin{split}b^{(k)}(b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E}))&=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}b(b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E}))+2r^{-1}\cancel{\mathcal{P}_{0}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{u}}\\
&=2\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}\Big(\partial_{(r}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{u)}-r^{-2}\partial_{(z}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{\bar{z})}+r^{-1}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{u}\Big)\\
&=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}\Big(\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}\partial_{r}b(\mathcal{E})_{u}+\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}\partial_{u}b(\mathcal{E})_{r}-\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-3,-4\rrbracket}(r^{-2}\partial_{z}b(\mathcal{E})_{\bar{z}})\\
&\quad-\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-4\rrbracket}(r^{-2}\partial_{\bar{z}}b(\mathcal{E})_{z})+2\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-2,-3\rrbracket}(r^{-1}b(\mathcal{E})_{u})\Big)\\
&=2\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-3\rrbracket}\Big(\partial_{(r}b(\mathcal{E})_{u)}-r^{-2}\partial_{(z}b(\mathcal{E})_{\bar{z})}+r^{-1}b(\mathcal{E})_{u}\Big)+2r^{-2}\cancel{\mathcal{P}_{-1}\partial_{(z}b(\mathcal{E})_{\bar{z})}}\end{split}

where the cancelled terms vanish because of (

)

\mathcal{P}_{0}b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E})_{u}=\mathcal{P}_{\{0\}\cap\llbracket k-1,-2\rrbracket}b(\mathcal{E})_{u}=0
.
. Therefore,

b^{(k)}(b^{(k)}(\mathcal{E}))=\mathcal{P}_{\llbracket k-1,-3\rrbracket}b(b(\mathcal{E}))=0\;,

which follows from the nilpotency of

.
