Remark B.2

(Projected box operator)
.

Let
\Pi_{k}
with
k\leq 0
be the projection in (
116
), and let
\Box^{(k)}
be the operator defined in (
122
). Then
\Box^{(k)}=\Pi_{k}\Box
.

Proof.

We observe that

\displaystyle\Box^{(k)}-\Pi_{k}\Box

\displaystyle=[b^{k},m_{1}^{k}]-\Pi_{k}[b,m_{1}]=\Pi_{k}(b\Pi_{k}m_{1}+\cancel{m_{1}\Pi_{k}b}-bm_{1}-\cancel{m_{1}b})=\Pi_{k}b\Pi_{k}^{\complement}m_{1}\;,

(247)

where we used the definition of
m_{1}^{(k)}
,
b^{(k)}
and the equation
\Pi_{k}m_{1}\Pi_{k}=\Pi_{k}m_{1}
(the color-stripped differential
m_{1}^{(k)}
inherits such identity from the commuting diagram (
98
) of the differential
B_{1}^{(k)}
). Thus, it suffices to prove that
\Pi_{k}b\Pi_{k}^{\complement}m_{1}(u)
vanishes for all
51
51

51

Notice that
\Pi_{k}b\Pi_{k}^{\complement}m_{1}(u)=0
for all
u\in K_{2}^{(k)}
, by degree, so there is no need to consider this case.

u\in\mathcal{K}^{(k)}
. This can be done using the solution (
116
) for
\Pi_{k}
and the properties of the projection
\mathcal{P}
listed in
appendix

A
. For instance, consider the case where
u=\Lambda\in K_{0}^{(k)}
:

\begin{split}\Pi_{k}b\Pi_{k}^{\complement}m_{1}(\Lambda)&=\pi_{\mathcal{N}^{k}}\nabla^{\mu}\pi_{(\mathcal{N}^{k})^{\complement}}\partial_{\mu}\Lambda=-\mathcal{P}_{N_{\Lambda}^{k}}\big\{\big(\partial_{u}\mathcal{P}_{(N_{A_{r}}^{k})^{\complement}}\partial_{r}\Lambda+r\leftrightarrow u\big)\\
&\quad-r^{-2}\big(\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathcal{P}_{(N_{A_{z}}^{k})^{\complement}}\partial_{z}\Lambda+z\leftrightarrow\bar{z}\big)+2r^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{(N_{A_{u}}^{k})^{\complement}}\partial_{u}\Lambda\big\}\\
&=-2\partial_{r}\partial_{u}\mathcal{P}_{1}\Lambda+2r^{-2}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathcal{P}_{\{1,2\}}\Lambda+2r^{-1}\partial_{u}\mathcal{P}_{1}\Lambda=0\;,\end{split}

(248)

where we used the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{n}\Lambda=0
for all positive
n
.
∎

B.2
\mathfrak{Kin}^{(k,f)}

In
section

5
, our goal was to define an
L_{\infty}
algebra to encode SDYM near
\mathcal{I}
by suitably truncating the
r
-expansion of the elements (
75
) in the initial graded vector space
\mathcal{X}
. We showed that the only way to achieve this is by defining subspaces
\mathcal{X}^{(k)}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
through the projection
\Pi_{k}
and the power selection sets in
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, on which the required
L_{\infty}
algebras can be constructed - see equations (
116
)-(
118
). In particular, the maxima of the power selection sets (which correspond to the fall-off) are fixed and common to all the slices
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, as we emphasize in
fig.

1
. Our goal here is to construct strict kinematic algebras by further projecting components of the
r
-expansion to zero.

To this end, we construct the refined slices
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
which are defined by projecting components of the
r
-expansion of
\psi\in\mathcal{X}
to zero, and introducing a projection that modifies the functional dependence of the gauge parameters
\Lambda\in X_{-1}
. Indeed, if one makes a gauge choice for the fields, the gauge choice imposes constraints on the gauge parameters. To clarify this point, let us consider an explicit example. As we show in
section

6.2.1
, one way to refine
\mathfrak{S}_{-1}
in order to strictify its kinematic algebra is to modify the fall-off of
A_{u}
from
0
to
-1
, i.e. to replace the power selection set of
A_{u}
as follows:

N_{A_{u}}^{(-1)}=\{-2,-1,0\}\ \rightarrow\ N_{A_{u}}^{(-1,f)}:=\{-2,-1\}\;.

(249)

Let
\Pi_{-1,f}
be the projection defined as in (
116
), but with the substitution above for the set
N_{A_{u}}^{(-1)}
. As expected, the triple
(\mathcal{X}^{(-1,f)},B_{1}^{(-1,f)},B_{2}^{(-1,f)})
constructed accordingly
52
52

52

That is, using the definition (
118
) with
\Pi_{-1}
replaced by
\Pi_{-1,f}
.
does not form an
L_{\infty}
algebra. Nevertheless, one can prove that the brackets
B_{i}^{(-1,f)}
satisfy the
L_{\infty}
relations up to terms proportional to
\partial_{u}\Lambda^{(0)}
. This means that enforcing
\Lambda^{(0)}
to be independent of
u
guarantees the restoration of an
L_{\infty}
algebra, thus resolving the problem. Therefore, we require that the projection
\Pi_{-1,f}
acts on
\Lambda\in X_{-1}
as

\Lambda(r,u,z,\bar{z})=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}r^{n}\Lambda^{(n)}(u,z,\bar{z})\ \xrightarrow{\Pi_{-1,f}}\ \Lambda^{(0)}(z,\bar{z})+\sum_{n=-2}^{-1}r^{n}\Lambda^{(n)}(u,z,\bar{z})

(250)

Physically, this procedure has a clear interpretation: the replacement (
249
) simply amounts to setting
A_{u}^{(0)}=0
, and the consistency of the gauge transformation then implies that
\partial_{u}\Lambda^{(0)}
vanishes.

Building on this reasoning and generalizing it, we now provide the explicit definition of the projector
\Pi_{k,f}
introduced in
section

6.2
, which is used to construct the refined slices
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
. The first ingredient that we need is the following collection of power selection sets:

\mathcal{N}_{k,f}\coloneqq\{N^{(k,f)}_{\Lambda},N^{(k,f)}_{A_{\mu}},N^{(k)}_{E_{\mu\nu}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}\}\;,

(251)

where the sets
N_{I}^{(k,f)}\subseteq N_{I}^{(k)}
,
I=\Lambda,A_{\mu}
, are defined as

\displaystyle N_{\Lambda}^{(k,f)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{k-1,\dots,f_{r}^{k}+1,0\}

(252)

\displaystyle N_{A_{\mu}}^{(k,f)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{k-2,\dots,f_{r}^{k}\}&\mu=r\\
\{k-1,\dots,f_{u}^{k}\}&\mu=u\\
\{k,\dots,f_{z}^{k}\}&\mu=z\\
\{k-1,\dots,f_{\bar{z}}^{k}\}&\mu=\bar{z}\end{cases}\;,

(
127
)

and the new variable fall-off are given by

f_{\mu}^{k}\coloneqq\max N_{A_{\mu}}^{(k,f)}\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{s.th.}\quad\begin{cases}k-2\leq f^{k}_{r}\leq-2\\
k-1\leq f^{k}_{u},f^{k}_{\bar{z}}\leq 0\\
k\leq f^{k}_{z}\leq 0\end{cases}.

(
128
)

The second ingredient is an operator
\mathfrak{D}_{k,f}
that generalizes (
250
), by appropriately modifying the functional dependence of the gauge parameter. To construct such operator, which of course depends on the modified fall-offs
f_{\mu}^{k}
, we need one more definition. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in\{u,z,\bar{z}\}
. We introduce a map
\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha|m}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}))
that acts on
g\in\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
as

\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha|m}(g)\coloneqq\sum_{n=-\infty}^{m}\mathcal{P}_{n}g+\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{n}g\Big|_{\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{n}g=0}\;.

(253)

We list below two examples to clarify how this map works:

\begin{split}\mathfrak{d}_{u|4}(g)&=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{4}r^{n}g^{(n)}(u,z,\bar{z})+\sum_{n=5}^{\infty}r^{n}g^{(n)}(z,\bar{z})\\
\mathfrak{d}_{z|-1}(\mathfrak{d}_{u|4}(g))&=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{-1}r^{n}g^{(n)}(u,z,\bar{z})+\sum_{n=0}^{4}r^{n}g^{(n)}(u,\bar{z})+\sum_{n=5}^{\infty}r^{n}g^{(n)}(\bar{z})\;.\end{split}

(254)

The operator
\mathfrak{D}_{k,f}
is then defined as

\mathfrak{D}_{k,f}\coloneqq\prod_{\alpha}\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha|f_{\alpha}^{k}}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}))\;.

(255)

At this point, we can finally state the definition of the desired projection from
\mathcal{X}
to the refined slice
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
(with
k\leq 0
and fall-off
f_{\mu}^{k}
):

\Pi_{k,f}(\psi)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathfrak{D}_{k,f}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{N}_{k,f}}(\psi)&\psi\in X_{-1}\\
\pi_{\mathcal{N}_{k,f}}(\psi)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(256)

To provide a couple of examples, we now explicitly write how
\Pi_{k,f}
projects the gauge fields when selecting the first two fall-offs from
table

4
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{k,f_{4}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=k+2}^{0}r^{n}\Lambda^{(n)}(z)+\sum_{n=k-1}^{k+1}r^{n}\Lambda^{(n)}(u,z,\bar{z})\in X_{-1}^{(k,f_{4})}

(257)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{k,f_{5}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{0}(u,\bar{z})+r^{k-1}\Lambda^{(k-1)}(u,z,\bar{z})\in X_{-1}^{(k,f_{5})}\;.

Thanks to the projection above, for any non positive integer
k
and choice of fall-off
f_{\mu}^{k}
satisfying (
128
), it is possible to construct a kinematic algebra
\mathfrak{Kin}^{(k,f)}
on the refined slice
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
following a procedure analogous to that presented in
section

6.1
. Hence, we can prove the following result.

Result B.3

(Strict kinematic algebra)
.

Let the
\mathrm{BV}_{\infty}^{\Box^{(k,f)}}
algebra
\mathfrak{Kin}^{(k,f)}
be the kinematic algebra of the refined slice
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
, for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}
and
f_{\mu}^{k}
satisfying (
128
). Then

\mathfrak{Kin}^{(k,f)}\text{\ is strict}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad f_{\mu}^{k}\text{\ satisfy}\quad\begin{cases}f_{r}^{k}+f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}<k\\
f_{r}^{k}+f_{u}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k-1\\
f_{r}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k\\
f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k+1\\
f_{r}^{k}+f_{u}^{k}<k\\
f_{r}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k\\
f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}<k+2\\
f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k+2\;.\end{cases}

(
130
)

Note that the system above is symmetric under
f_{r}^{k}\leftrightarrow f_{z}^{k}-2
and
f_{u}^{k}\leftrightarrow f_{\bar{z}}^{k}
.

Proof.

The kinematic algebra
\mathfrak{Kin}^{(k,f)}
of a refined slice
\mathfrak{S}_{k,f}
with fall-off
f_{\mu}^{k}
is strict if the corresponding trilinear bracket
\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}
vanishes (see discussion at the end of
section

3.3
). Thus, proving the above result is equivalent to finding the conditions such that

\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=0\quad\forall u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in\mathcal{K}^{(k,f)}

(258)

To study when this happens, recall that

\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=\Pi_{k,f}\theta_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})=\Pi_{k,f}\theta_{3}(\Pi_{k,f}u_{1},\Pi_{k,f}u_{2},\Pi_{k,f}u_{3})\;.

(259)

We start by considering
\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}
acting on three gauge fields. The
r
-component is

\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}({\mathcal{A}}_{1},{\mathcal{A}}_{2},{\mathcal{A}}_{3})_{r}=\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}}\big[6r^{-2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{[1\,r}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{z}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{2\,z}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{3]\,\bar{z}}\big)\big]

(260)

To determine when this expression vanishes, we employ a generalisation of identity (
166g
) of
appendix

A
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
L,M_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, one can show that
53
53

53

The procedure is analogous to the proof of (
166g
).

\mathcal{P}_{L}\Big(\prod_{i}\mathcal{P}_{M_{i}}g_{i}\Big)=0\quad\forall g_{i}\in C_{r}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad L\cap\sum_{i}M_{i}=\emptyset\;.

(261)

Thus, we conclude that (
260
) vanishes for all
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}\in K_{1}^{(k,f)}
iff

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}\cap\big(N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}+N_{A_{z}}^{(k,f)}+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{(k,f)}\big)

(262)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,f_{r}^{k}+2\rrbracket\cap\big(\llbracket k-2,f_{r}^{k}\rrbracket+\llbracket k,f_{z}^{k}\rrbracket+\llbracket k-1,f_{\bar{z}}^{k}\rrbracket\big)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,f_{r}+2\rrbracket\cap\llbracket 3k-3,f_{r}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}\rrbracket\;.

We note that
f_{r}^{k}+2\geq 3k-3
, since by hypothesis
54
54

54

See eq. (
128
).

f_{r}^{k}\geq k-2
and
k\leq 0
. Thus, the above equation is true iff

f_{r}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k\;.

(263)

Repeating the same procedure for the other components, we find that

\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3})=0\quad\forall\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}\in K_{1}^{(k,f)}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}\sum_{\mu\neq r}f_{\mu}^{k}<k+1\\
\sum_{\mu\neq u}f_{\mu}^{k}<k\\
\sum_{\mu\neq z}f_{\mu}^{k}<k-1\\
\sum_{\mu\neq\bar{z}}f_{\mu}^{k}<k\end{cases}\;,

(264)

which prove the first four inequalities of the system in (
130
). Now, let us turn to the study of
\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
, where
\mathcal{E}\in K_{2}^{(k,f)}
. Its
ru
-component is

\displaystyle\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(\mathcal{E},{\mathcal{A}}_{1},{\mathcal{A}}_{2})_{ru}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{ru}}^{(k)}}\big\{2r^{-2}\big[\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{uz}}^{(k)}}\mathcal{E}_{uz}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{[1\,r}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{2]\,\bar{z}}\big)

(265)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}^{(k)}}\mathcal{E}_{r\bar{z}}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{u}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{[1\,u}\big)\big(\mathcal{P}_{N_{A_{z}}^{(k,f)}}\mathcal{A}_{2]\,z}\big)\big]\big\}

The above expression vanishes
\forall\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\in K_{1}^{(k,f)}
and
\forall\mathcal{E}\in K_{2}^{(k,f)}
iff

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}^{(k)}\cap\big(N_{E_{uz}}^{(k)}+N_{A_{r}}^{(k,f)}+N_{A_{\bar{z}}}^{(k,f)}\big)

(266)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,0\rrbracket\cap\big(\llbracket k,0\rrbracket+\llbracket k-2,f_{r}^{k}\rrbracket+\llbracket k-1,f_{\bar{z}}^{k}\rrbracket\big)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,0\rrbracket\cap\llbracket 3k-3,f_{r}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}\rrbracket

and

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{2}N_{E_{ru}}^{(k)}\cap\big(N_{E_{r\bar{z}}}^{(k)}+N_{A_{u}}^{(k,f)}+N_{A_{z}}^{(k,f)}\big)

(267)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,0\rrbracket\cap\big(\llbracket k-2,-2\rrbracket+\llbracket k-1,f_{u}^{k}\rrbracket+\llbracket k,f_{z}^{k}\rrbracket\big)

\displaystyle=\llbracket k,0\rrbracket\cap\llbracket 3k-3,f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}-2\rrbracket

Since
k\leq 0
, the above equation hold, respectviley, iff

f_{r}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k\quad\text{and}\quad f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}\leq k+2\;.

(268)

Applying the same procedure to the other two independent components, we obtain that

\theta_{3}^{(k,f)}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})=0\quad\forall\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\in K_{1}^{(k,f)},\mathcal{E}\in K_{2}^{(k,f)}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}f_{r}^{k}+f_{u}^{k}<k\\
f_{r}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k\\
f_{u}^{k}+f_{z}^{k}<k+2\\
f_{z}^{k}+f_{\bar{z}}^{k}<k+2\end{cases},

(269)

thus completing the proof of the system in (
130
).
∎

Appendix C
Homotopy transfer

In this Appendix, we introduce the notion of homotopy transfer, a mathematical tool used to transfer algebraic (non-linear) structure from one cochain complex to another. In the following, we illustrate how homotopy transfer works for
L_{\infty}
algebras. However, one can extend this concept to other algebras such as
C_{\infty}
or BV
∞
algebras.

Given an
L_{\infty}
algebra
(\mathcal{X},B_{1},B_{n})
defined on a graded vector space
\mathcal{X}
with differential
B_{1}
and multilinear maps
B_{n}
with
n>1
, we wish to find an
L_{\infty}
algebra structure on a subspace
\bar{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
equipped with a differential
\bar{B}_{1}
. To that end, following our discussion on quasi-isomorphisms in
7.1
, we introduce two cochain maps: a projection map
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\bar{\mathcal{X}}
and an inclusion map
\iota:\bar{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}
, i.e.,

\pi\circ B_{1}=\bar{B}_{1}\circ\pi\;,\;\;\;\iota\circ\bar{B}_{1}=B_{1}\circ\iota\;,

(270)

which trivially obey

\pi\circ\iota=1_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}}\;,

(271)

where
1_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}}
is the identity map on
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
. From now on we drop the
\circ
symbol to denote composition to simplify notation. Homotopy transfer further requires that these maps obey

\iota\pi-1_{\mathcal{X}}=[B_{1},h]\;,

(272)

which tells us that the inclusion map
\iota
is the inverse of the projection map
\pi

up to homotopy
, namely that the failure of
\iota
to be the inverse of
\pi
is governed by the differential
B_{1}
and a linear
homotopy map

h
of degree
-1
that obeys the so-called side conditions

\pi h=h\iota=h^{2}=0\;.

(273)

Even though all the above relations only involve linear maps, these relations, together with the multilinear brackets of the
B_{n}
of the algebra defined on
\mathcal{X}
, lead to the definition of the multilinear maps
\bar{B}_{n}:\bar{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes n}\to\bar{\mathcal{X}}
that define a new
L_{\infty}
algebra on the subspace
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
which we present here up to trilinear order:

\begin{split}\bar{B}_{2}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2})&=\pi B_{2}(\iota\bar{x}_{1},\iota\bar{x}_{2})\;,\\
\bar{B}_{3}(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\bar{x}_{3})&=\pi B_{3}(\iota\bar{x}_{1},\iota\bar{x}_{2},\iota\bar{x}_{3})+\pi B_{2}(hB_{2}(\iota\bar{x}_{1},\iota\bar{x}_{2}),\iota\bar{x}_{3})\\
&\quad+(-1)^{\bar{x}_{1}(\bar{x}_{2}+\bar{x}_{2})}\pi B_{2}(hB_{2}(\iota\bar{x}_{2},\iota\bar{x}_{3}),\iota\bar{x}_{1})\\
&\quad+(-1)^{\bar{x}_{3}(\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2})}\pi B_{2}(hB_{2}(\iota\bar{x}_{3},\iota\bar{x}_{1}),\iota\bar{x}_{2})\;.\end{split}

(274)

For a general expression for all maps
\bar{B}_{n}
and a more detailed explanation of homotopy transfer refer to
[
70
]
. Notice that, even without a map
B_{3}
in our original algebra defined on
\mathcal{X}
, in principle, a non-trivial three-bracket
\bar{B}_{3}
may exist. This implies that if one starts with a strict algebra on
\mathcal{X}
, the transferred algebraic structure on a subspace
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
is not generally strict. In the following, we perform homotopy transfer of self-dual Yang-Mills theory to the slices that we considered in the main body of the paper.

C.1
Homotopy map

Result C.1
.

The homotopy map
h\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is given by

\displaystyle h(A)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}A_{r}

(275)

\displaystyle h(E)_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\partial_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{-1}E_{r\bar{z}}

\displaystyle h(E)_{u}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial^{2}}\left[2\partial_{u}E_{ru}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}\partial_{z}E_{r\bar{z}}-\partial_{\bar{z}}E_{uz}\right)\right]

\displaystyle h(E)_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\partial_{u}}\left(\partial_{z}h(E)_{u}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}E_{uz}\right)

\displaystyle h(E)_{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{2\partial_{r}}E_{r\bar{z}}\;,

where
\mathbb{N}^{+}\coloneqq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and
\partial^{2}
is defined in (
85
).

Proof.

We want to prove that the map
h
above satisfies

[B_{1},h]\psi=\left(\iota\pi-1_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\psi\;.

(276)

for all
\psi\in\mathcal{X}
. To do so, it is useful to first study how the projector
\mathcal{P}
commutes with the inverse of the derivatives. For any set
M\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following identities hold:

\mathcal{P}_{M\setminus\{0\}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}=\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\mathcal{P}_{T_{-1}M\setminus\{-1\}}\;,\quad\mathcal{P}_{M}\frac{1}{\partial_{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{\partial_{\alpha}}\mathcal{P}_{M}\;.

(277)

The second equation is trivial, since
\partial_{\alpha}^{-1}
leaves the
r
-expansion invariant. To prove the first one, we notice that for any integer
m\neq 0

\mathcal{P}_{m}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\delta_{m,n+1}}{n+1}r^{n+1}f^{(n)}=\frac{1}{m}r^{m}f^{(m-1)}=\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}(r^{m-1}f^{(m-1)})=\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\mathcal{P}_{m-1}f\;,

(278)

where
\mathcal{P}_{m}
is the projection defined in (
168
). In particular, this implies that expressions of the form
\mathcal{P}_{M\setminus\{0\}}\partial_{r}^{-1}f
are well defined. For instance,
h(A)
in (
275
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle h(A)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}A_{r}=-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{m}\mathcal{P}_{m}(rA_{r})\;.

(279)

Equation (
276
) with
\psi=\Lambda\in X_{-1}
reads

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{r}\Lambda=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\Lambda=\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\Lambda=\left(\iota\pi-1_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\Lambda\;,

(280)

where we used (
166b
) and the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\Lambda=\iota\pi\Lambda
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda=\Lambda
. For
\psi=A_{\mu}\in X_{0}
the right hand side of (
276
) has components

\displaystyle\left(\iota\pi-1_{\mathcal{X}}\right)A_{r}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{<0}\setminus\{-1\}}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}\right)A_{r}=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}A_{r}

(281)

\displaystyle\left(\iota\pi-1_{\mathcal{X}}\right)A_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}\right)A_{\alpha}=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}A_{\alpha}\;,

while the components of the left hand side are

\displaystyle([B_{1},h]A)_{r}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}A_{r}+\frac{2}{\partial_{\bar{z}}}\mathcal{P}_{-1}\partial_{[r}A_{\bar{z}]}=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}}A_{r}

(282)

\displaystyle([B_{1},h]A)_{u}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}A_{r}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{4}{\partial^{2}}\bigg[\partial_{u}\partial_{[r}A_{u]}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}\partial_{z}\partial_{[r}A_{\bar{z}]}-\partial_{\bar{z}}\partial_{[u}A_{z]}-\partial_{u}\partial_{[z}A_{\bar{z}]}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\left\{\frac{2}{\partial^{2}}\left[\left(-\partial_{r}\partial_{u}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}A_{r}-A_{u}\right)\right]-\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}A_{r}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}A_{u}

\displaystyle([B_{1},h]A)_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\left[\frac{1}{\partial_{u}}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{u}\partial_{z}A_{r}-\partial_{z}A_{u}-2\partial_{[u}A_{z]}\right)-\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{z}A_{r}\right]=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}A_{z}

\displaystyle([B_{1},h]A)_{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{r}-2\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}\frac{1}{\partial_{r}}\partial_{[r}A_{\bar{z}]}=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{N}^{+}}A_{\bar{z}}\;.

This proves that the homotopy map
h
in (
275
) satisfies its defining equation (
276
).
∎

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\textit{commutes}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Jac}(\bar{B}_{2})=\pi\mathrm{Jac}(B_{2})\iota

(283)

Moreover, if (
283
) and the second condition
h\iota=0
are satisfied and
\mathrm{Jac}(B_{2})=0
, then
\bar{B}_{3}=0
.

Proof.

The above diagram commutes iff
\iota\bar{B}_{2}=B_{2}\iota
, which is equivalent to
\iota\pi B_{2}\iota=B_{2}\iota
(recalling that by definition
\bar{B}_{2}=\pi B_{2}\iota
). In other words,
\mathrm{Im}(B_{2}\iota)\subseteq\mathrm{Ker}[B_{1},h]
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}(\bar{B}_{2})(\bar{\psi}_{1},\bar{\psi}_{2},\bar{\psi}_{3})

\displaystyle=\bar{B}_{2}(\bar{B}_{2}(\bar{\psi}_{1},\bar{\psi}_{2}),\bar{\psi}_{3})+{\dots}=\pi B_{2}(\iota\pi B_{2}(\iota\bar{\psi}_{1},\iota\bar{\psi}_{2}),\iota\bar{\psi}_{3})+\dots

(284)

\displaystyle=\pi B_{2}(B_{2}(\iota\bar{\psi}_{1},\iota\bar{\psi}_{2}),\iota\bar{\psi}_{3})+{\dots}=\pi\mathrm{Jac}(B_{2})(\iota\bar{\psi}_{1},\iota\bar{\psi}_{2},\iota\bar{\psi}_{3})

for all
\bar{\psi}_{1},\bar{\psi}_{2},\bar{\psi}_{3}\in\bar{\mathcal{X}}
.
From this, we see that if
B_{2}
satisfies the Jacobi identity, then also
\bar{B}_{2}
does. In this case
[\bar{B}_{1},\bar{B}_{3}]=0
, that does not imply, in general,
\bar{B}_{3}=0
. However, from

\displaystyle\bar{B}_{3}(\bar{\psi}_{1},\bar{\psi}_{2},\bar{\psi}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pi B_{2}(hB_{2}(\iota\bar{\psi}_{1},\iota\bar{\psi}_{2})\iota\bar{\psi}_{3})=\pi B_{2}(h\iota\pi B_{2}(\iota\bar{\psi}_{1},\iota\bar{\psi}_{2})\iota\bar{\psi}_{3})\;,

(285)

we deduce that
\bar{B}_{3}
vanishes if the homotopy map satisfies the side condition
h\iota=0
.
∎

Result C.3
.

\bar{B}_{3}=0

Proof.

Thanks to the above lemma and since
B_{2}
satisfies the Jacobi identity and the side condition
h\iota=0
is true, it is sufficient to prove that
\iota\pi B_{2}\iota=B_{2}\iota
. For example,
\forall\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\bar{\Lambda}^{2}\in\bar{X}_{-1}

\displaystyle\iota\pi B_{2}(\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2})

\displaystyle=-\iota\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}[\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2}]=-\iota\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}[\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2}]

(286)

\displaystyle=-[\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2}]=-[\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2}]=B_{2}(\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{1},\iota\bar{\Lambda}^{2})

where in the second equality we used
\bar{\Lambda}^{i}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\bar{\Lambda}^{i}
while in the third we used (
166e
) with
\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}+\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}=\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}
. All the other cases can be proved in the same way, recalling (
166b
) and that
I_{i}+I_{2}=I_{2}
for
i=1,2
, with
I_{1}=\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}
and
I_{2}=\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}\setminus\{-1\}
. In particular,
\bar{B}_{2}=B_{2}|_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}}
where
\bar{\mathcal{X}}
is viewed as a subset of
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

C.3
Kinematic algebra from homotopy transfer

Since the operators in (
150
) are color blinded, the linear structure of the algebra
(\mathcal{K},m_{1},m_{2})
obtained by colour stripping and the corresponding homotopy transfer are preserved.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{K}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{K}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{K}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{m}_{1}}

(287)

where
\bar{m}_{1}\coloneqq\pi m_{1}\iota
. The above observation together with
\iota\bar{B}_{2}=b_{2}\iota
imply
\iota\bar{m}_{2}=m_{2}\iota
. Moreover, we define

\bar{b}:=\pi b\iota,

(288)

Lemma C.4
.

\bar{\Box}=\pi\Box\iota

Proof.

From the homotopy transfer (
287
) we know
\bar{m}_{1}\pi=\pi m_{1}
and
\iota\bar{m}_{1}=m_{1}\iota
, so

\displaystyle\bar{\Box}=\bar{m}_{1}\bar{b}+\bar{b}\bar{m}_{1}=\bar{m}_{1}\pi b\iota+\pi b\iota\bar{m}_{1}=\pi m_{1}b\iota+\pi bm_{1}\iota=\pi\Box\iota

(289)

∎

Lemma C.5
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

commutes

(290)

Proof.

The above diagram commutes if
\iota\bar{b}=b\iota
. From the definition of
\bar{b}
, this is equivalent to
\iota\pi b\iota=b\iota
, i.e.
\iota\pi=1_{\mathcal{K}}
on
\mathrm{Im}(b\iota)
. We make use of (
169
) to conclude that
\forall\bar{A}\in\bar{K}_{1}

\displaystyle\iota\pi b\iota\bar{A}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\left(\nabla^{\mu}\bar{A}_{\mu}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\left(\nabla_{\{r}\bar{A}_{u\}}-r^{-2}\nabla_{\{z}\bar{A}_{\bar{z}\}}\right)

(291)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{\leq 0}}\left[\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{<}\setminus\{-1\}}\left(\nabla_{\{r}\bar{A}_{u\}}-r^{-2}\nabla_{\{z}\bar{A}_{\bar{z}\}}\right)+2\mathcal{P}_{-1}(r^{-1}\bar{A}_{u})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{<}\setminus\{-1\}}\left(\nabla_{\{r}\bar{A}_{u\}}-r^{-2}\nabla_{\{z}\bar{A}_{\bar{z}\}}\right)+2\mathcal{P}_{-1}(r^{-1}\bar{A}_{u})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\{r}\bar{A}_{u\}}-r^{-2}\nabla_{\{z}\bar{A}_{\bar{z}\}}=b\iota\bar{A}

∎

Result C.6
.

\bar{b}_{2}=\pi b_{2}\iota

Proof.

Result (
C.3
) implies
\iota\bar{m}_{2}=m_{2}\iota
, since colour stripping does not affect the analogous property for
B_{2}
. Using the definition of
\bar{m}_{2}
(
288
), this relation translates to
\iota\pi m_{2}\iota=m_{2}\iota
. Recalling Lemma (
C.5
), we conclude that
\iota\pi=1_{\mathcal{K}}
on both
\mathrm{Im}(m_{2}\iota)
and
\mathrm{Im}(b\iota)
. Thus, the bracket
\bar{b}_{2}
, that is defined from
\bar{b}
and
\bar{m}_{2}
, is

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bar{b}\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2})-\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{b}\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2})-(-1)^{|\bar{u}_{1}|}\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{b}\bar{u}_{2})

(292)

\displaystyle=\pi b\iota\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-\pi m_{2}(\iota\pi b\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-(-1)^{|\bar{u}_{1}|}\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\pi b\iota\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=\pi b_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})

∎

Result C.7
.

\bar{\theta}_{3}=\pi\theta_{3}\iota

Proof.

The Poisson identity is:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Poiss}}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2},\bar{u}_{3})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2}),\bar{u}_{3})-(-)^{\bar{u}_{1}(\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{u}_{3})}\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{u}_{2},\bar{u}_{3}),\bar{u}_{1})

(293)

\displaystyle-(-)^{\bar{u}_{3}(\bar{u}_{1}+\bar{u}_{2})}\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{u}_{3},\bar{u}_{1}),\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=[\bar{m}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{3}](\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2},\bar{u}_{3})

\forall\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2},\bar{u}_{3}\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}
. The first term in the first line above is

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2}),\bar{u}_{3})=\pi b_{2}(\iota\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2}),\iota\bar{u}_{3})=\pi b_{2}(m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2}),\iota\bar{u}_{3})

(294)

where we used
\iota\pi=1_{\mathcal{K}}
on
\mathrm{Im}(m_{2}\iota)
. Note that we also have
\iota\pi=1_{\mathcal{K}}
on
\mathrm{Im}(b_{2}\iota)
, since

\displaystyle\iota\pi b_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=\iota\pi bm_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-\iota\pi m_{2}(b\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-(-)^{\bar{u}_{1}}\iota\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},b\iota\bar{u}_{2})

(295)

\displaystyle=\iota\pi b\iota\bar{m}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})-\iota\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{b}u_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-(-)^{\bar{u}_{1}}\iota\pi m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{b}\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=b\iota\bar{m}_{2}(u_{1},u_{2})-m_{2}(\iota\bar{b}u_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-(-)^{\bar{u}_{1}}m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{b}\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=bm_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-m_{2}(b\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})-(-)^{\bar{u}_{1}}m_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},b\iota\bar{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=b_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{1},\iota\bar{u}_{2})

\forall\bar{u}_{1},\bar{u}_{2}\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}
, where we used
\iota\bar{m}_{2}=m_{2}\iota
and (
290
). Thus, the second term in the first line of (
293
) is

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{2}(\bar{b}_{2}(\bar{u}_{2},\bar{u}_{3}),\bar{u}_{1})=\pi m_{2}(\iota\pi b_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{2},\iota\bar{u}_{3}),\iota\bar{u}_{1})=\pi m_{2}(b_{2}(\iota\bar{u}_{2},\iota\bar{u}_{3}),\iota\bar{u}_{1})

(296)

The third term in the Poissonator can be treated analogously; putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathrm{Poiss}}=\pi\mathrm{Poiss}\iota=\pi[m_{1},\theta_{3}]\iota=\pi m_{1}\theta_{3}\iota+\pi\theta_{3}m_{1}\iota=\bar{m}_{1}\pi\theta_{3}\iota+\pi\theta_{3}\iota\bar{m}_{1}

(297)

∎
